- **Not done**: no live provider was called (no key), so OpenRouter's actual answers (headers accepted, `/models` shape, model-not-found status) are as documented, not observed; the screens were checked as rendered widgets, not in the running app; Gemini, Groq and Together addresses and key links come from their public docs and are unchecked.

## G6 (notarization pipeline and the Linux desktop build)

Details, setup and checklists in [docs/desktop-release.md](docs/desktop-release.md).

- **`scripts/release-macos.sh` is the pipeline; `package-macos-app.sh` is a one-line wrapper** so `release.yml` is unchanged. Modes come from the environment: no `MACOS_SIGN_IDENTITY` (or `--dry-run`) is ad-hoc and never talks to Apple; an identity signs; notary credentials (keychain profile, App Store Connect API key, or Apple ID) notarize. `REQUIRE_NOTARIZE=1` makes a half-done release an error. Credentials are only ever read from the environment or the keychain.
- **Signed inside out, no `--deep`**, with the hardened runtime and a timestamp on every piece (notarization rejects anything else). `notarytool`'s exit code isn't trusted: the status is read from its plist output and a non-Accepted result prints Apple's log. The app and then the dmg are notarized and stapled; the zip is made from the stapled app.
- **Entitlements stay at `app-sandbox = false`.** The app is unsandboxed, so network, spawning the sidecar and `sandbox-exec` need nothing; the Keychain is reached by running `/usr/bin/security`; there's no JIT (Flutter AOT, Monty is an interpreter); everything inside shares one identity so library validation holds. Table in the doc.
- **`scripts/verify-macos-app.sh`** checks structure and signature at three levels (adhoc, developer-id, notarized), and the pipeline runs it on the app, the app inside the mounted dmg, and the unzipped zip.
- **Linux formats: `.tar.gz` and `.deb`** (lowest friction: only `dpkg-deb`; the deb declares GTK). AppImage (GTK isn't really portable unbundled, FUSE 2, a download at build time) and Flatpak (sandbox permissions for the daemon) wait. Layout: `tern_desktop` and `tern` in one folder, which the tray's Open tern and `findTern` both rely on; `bundledTern` now returns `<dir>/tern` on Linux. Desktop entry `dev.tern.tern_desktop.desktop`, icon name `tern`, taken from the macOS icon PNGs and `tern-icon.svg`. No libsecret: tokens are a 0600 file on Linux. The GTK window title is now "tern".
- **`desktop-release.yml` runs on `desktop-v*` tags and manual dispatch**, not `v*`, because `release.yml` creates the release for `v*`. It ends in a draft release, never creates a tag, and builds only (artifacts) without a tag.
- **Not verified**: Apple signing and notarization (no credentials), `flutter build linux` and everything that runs on Linux (no Linux box). Local checks: the dry run end to end on a Mac, the Linux packaging against a stub bundle, shellcheck, actionlint, `flutter analyze`/`test`.

## Script grants: allow one python script for a run (shell)

- **The class is one command shape, parsed strictly.** `python3 <ws file>.py <plain args>` over the alphabet `[A-Za-z0-9 _./+,:=@%-]`, no options, no wrappers, `network` explicitly off, script canonicalised into the workspace and a regular file, interpreter outside every sandbox-writable root. Everything else keeps today's behaviour (still destructive, forced ask, same allowlist patterns). Chosen over "parse like a shell": a stricter lexer can't disagree with `sh`. The cost is that most of what an agent writes by habit (`-c`, heredocs, `| head`, `2>&1`) doesn't qualify, so the tool description, the data analyst's persona and the card all say so and steer to `write_file` + `python3 file.py args`. A pipe is pointless anyway: output is already captured and capped.
- **`Tool::grant_key` for shell, planned per call.** `Shell::policy` plans the call by (run id, call id), as `connector_test` does: resolved interpreter and script, hash, key. `grant_key` only returns that plan's key; `call` takes exactly its own plan, re-hashes the script, re-resolves the interpreter, refuses with "changed since it was approved; nothing was run", and runs the *planned* interpreter and canonical script path rather than the typed text. A call without a plan has no key. Plans go away with `forget_plans` (call won't run, run ended). Key = sha256(interpreter canonical + as found, script canonical, sha256(script), args, workspace) with a readable prefix; `tern grants` shows `key:python script <path>#<digest>` with an explanation.
- **Imports are not covered, and the card says so.** Hashing sibling `.py` files is not sound (packages, `sys.path`, `.pth`, `__pycache__`), so only the entry file is hashed. Same for data files: reading one doesn't taint.
- **The sandbox gate is a probe, not detection.** `HostBackend::verify` runs a canary through the production wrapper (positive control inside the workspace; a write outside that must fail and leave no file; with the network off a connect to a loopback listener that must be refused, by a probe tool that must work unsandboxed). It is the only thing that lets an approval be waived on the sandbox's account. Cached per (policy, workspace) for 10 min (30 s on failure), exposed in `tern doctor`, `tern security audit` and `/v1/info.sandbox`. Gate = verified AND network off AND untainted AND not readonly. Docker is never verified (can't be probed from the host; its flags aren't a probe), `confine = "off"` never is, a nested sandbox (exit 71) fails the positive control and so is not verified: fail closed. `SandboxBackend::verify` defaults to not verified so a new backend must opt in.
- **Ledger.** `GrantKey` gained `note` (card line) and `audit` (JSON). A call that runs on a scoped grant, nobody asked, now writes an `approval` entry with `decision: "approved_by_grant"`, the key, and `audit` (script path, sha256, interpreter, args, sandbox state). This applies to every tool's key grants, not only shell. `ApprovalRequest.notice` / `ApiApproval.notice` carry the host's own line to cards ("Imports from other files aren't covered", "Not auto-approved: ..."), never offered in a tainted run.
- **Not done.** No new autonomy level or per-agent flag; defaults for other shell commands unchanged. `write_file` in the workspace is already a host-granted write (no prompt), so the agent writing its scripts doesn't ask; no extra grant was needed. Original data files are not protected from scripts yet (a future read-only mount or copy-on-write area). A `ledger` row for grant use inside python scripts' sub-calls isn't added.
- **Starter.** The data analyst gets the Monty `python` tool (safe, no approvals, for simple CSV/JSON statistics) beside `shell`, and a persona line: one script per question, written with `write_file`, run as `python3 file.py args`.
- **Found while testing.** Opening a script that is a fifo blocks forever: `read_script` checks the path is a regular file and opens with `O_NONBLOCK` before the handle check.
## Sub-agent context isolation: bounded reports, `agent_result` (2026-11-04)

Question: does a sub-agent's input and output flow back into the parent's context? Measured first, then fixed what was loose.

- **Already isolated, by design (unchanged).** A child is its own session (`source = "subagent"`) and `Store::run_context` builds a run's context from its own session only, so the child's messages, tool calls, tool outputs and reasoning never enter the parent's prompt. What reaches the parent is the `agent_spawn` handle (about 200 bytes), the `agent_wait` JSON (status, iterations, tokens, run id and the child's last assistant text) or the same text as an `inbox` message, and `agent_status` lines. Journal steps, the activity feed and the events stream are separate from model context. Compaction treats a delivered report as any other message. Measured with the mock provider (a child making three 50 KB `read_file` calls, then a 40,000-character report): before, the parent's second model call carried 17,279 bytes of messages (the 16 KB cap plus framing) while the child's last call carried 98,983 bytes; the child's work never reached the parent.
- **What was loose, and is now fixed.** The report cap was a fixed 16,384 bytes (about 4,000 tokens per child, 12 children = 48,000 tokens), not configurable, and the cut left no way to get the rest ("[... cut at N bytes]"). Now `[subagents].max_result_chars` (default 8,000 characters, about 2,000 tokens; minimum 200) bounds the report in `Finished.result`. A cut report ends in `[... report truncated: showing the first N of M characters. Fetch the rest with agent_result {"agent": "<label>", "offset": N}]`, and `Finished.result_chars` records M. The same run now sends the parent 9,024 bytes of messages. The full text stays in the child's session.
- **`agent_result {agent, offset?, limit?}`** (parent side, read effect, no approval) returns a slice of a finished child's final text, at most `max_result_chars` per call, with `next_offset` or `complete: true`. It reads the child's last assistant message from its session; a running child is an error that points at `agent_wait`. Offsets are characters, so a slice never splits a UTF-8 sequence. It is in `AGENT_TOOLS`, so a child gets it below the depth limit, like the other parent-side tools.
- **Prompts.** The child role now says its final message is all the caller gets (not files, tool output or reasoning, and only the start of a long report), to keep it to a page, not to paste contents or logs, and to put long material in a workspace file and name it. The parent's Sub-agents section says reports are cut at the configured number, to ask for short ones, and about `agent_result`.
- **Taint, a gap closed.** Mail from a tainted run already taints and frames the reader (inbox step). But `agent_wait` delivers the same report and did not: a clean parent reading a tainted child's report through `agent_wait` stayed clean. `agent_wait` and `agent_result` now add `"tainted": true` to their reply when any delivered child run is tainted, and the `agent_*` tools turn that into `ToolResult.untrusted`, so the agent loop wraps the result in `<untrusted>` and taints the reader exactly as for any other untrusted tool result (journaled, replay-stable). Nothing else about taint changed (a tainted parent's children still start tainted; tainted mail still taints through the inbox).
- **Kept as is.** The child's ledger entries and journal steps (inspectable in the run inspector as before), cancellation cascades, delegation approvals and autonomy ceilings, `agent_mail` semantics (the `finished` mail still carries the report, now cut), and the desktop agents card (it reads `ChildRun.result_preview` and child sessions, not the capped text). `NOT_IN_SCRIPTS` is unchanged (`agent_result` is read-only, like `agent_status`).
- **Mock provider request log.** `TERN_MOCK_REQUEST_LOG=<file>` appends one JSON line per model call (first user message, message count, serialized bytes, token estimate), so a test can measure what a run's context holds. Used by the s1 tests.
- **Tests**: `crates/tern/tests/s1.rs` (a noisy child leaves the parent under 10,000 bytes against a 99 KB child context; the child's three tool steps, four llm steps and full report stay in its session and journal; `agent_result` fetches the tail and only then does the parent hold it; `max_result_chars = 1000` shrinks the bound; a short report is untouched), `subagents::tests` in the binary (cut text and marker, slice-by-slice fetch to `complete`, unknown label, a tainted child marks both `agent_wait` and `agent_result`), `tern-tools` (`tainted` makes a result untrusted), and the existing cancel and crash tests, unchanged.
- **Residual risks.** The bound is per report: a parent with 12 children can still receive 12 x 8,000 characters in one `agent_wait` (about 24,000 tokens); lower `max_result_chars` for fan-outs that wide. An `agent_wait` that returns several reports taints the reader if any one of them is tainted (not per report). A child whose last assistant message is empty (it stopped on a tool call) has no result text to fetch. Characters, not tokens, are the unit; for CJK text a character costs more tokens than the 4-per-token estimate.

## Several saved servers and fallback lists (desktop)

Two remaining desktop items (docs/gui-spec.md §7.5). Dart/Flutter only; no Rust or API change.

- **Servers.** `desktop.json` keeps `servers` (a list of paired servers) plus `connection` (the one in use, still written so an older app finds it). "This Mac" is `LocalProfile`, always present, never in the list, can't be removed. Each `RemoteProfile` has an `id` and its own Keychain/file-token `account` (`server:<id>`); `Connections` (state/connection.dart) does add (`connect`), re-pair (`repair`), `rename`, `remove`, `switchTo`, `status` and a token-free `test`. One change runs at a time (`_one`), so quick clicks can't leave prefs and connection on different servers.
- **Migration.** A file with only the old single `connection` becomes the first list entry. It keeps its token (its account stays the URL, read from the old place), and the old `read` unread marks move to it. Unreadable files mean "not chosen yet", as before.
- **Nothing crosses servers.** A switch sets the store to null for a frame (the old Home, its streams and caches are disposed, which also avoids two macOS menu bars at once) and then builds a new `TernStore` for the one endpoint. Unread marks are `read_by_server`, since channel ids repeat. A token is only read through the entry's own account, and endpoints are built from one entry's URL and token together.
- **Security.** `remoteUrlProblem` (tern_client): https required unless the host is loopback (only the literal `localhost`, 127/8 and `::1`: other names, even `*.localhost`, are left to the OS resolver, which may send them to DNS), no userinfo/query/fragment. Enforced in `parseServerInput`, `TernClient.pair`/`probe`, `Connections`, `endpointFor` and `checkServer`; a server saved as plain http by an older app is shown as "Can't be used", never contacted. Authenticated requests to a remote core no longer follow redirects (any 3xx is an error), so a token can't be forwarded elsewhere. Changing a server's address means pairing again; the old token is deleted only after the new pairing worked and is never sent to the new address. Removing a server deletes its token (the device stays registered on the server until revoked there, and the dialog says so). Using this computer no longer deletes a server's token (the old "Run on this Mac instead" did, when there was only one).
- **Test before saving.** `TernClient.probe` asks `/healthz` (no auth, no token) with the pin if the link has one; results are plain sentences (`serverErrorText`: wrong code, not a tern server, certificate mismatch, unreachable, redirected). Saved servers are checked with their own token (`checkServer`, 6 s timeout) for the dots in the switcher and manager.
- **Fallbacks.** `ModelChainEditor` (ui/model_chain_editor.dart): rows PRIMARY / THEN TRY, provider badge, drag handle plus up/down buttons, add via the existing searchable list, tap to replace, remove (the last can't go), and the T6a rule in two sentences. Same stored format as T6a: `default_model` is written through the existing `PUT /v1/config/settings/default_model` as a string or list (`setDefaultModelChain`, owner only; a chat device isn't offered the button). The default agent's sheet writes changed fallbacks there too, since `PATCH /v1/agents/tern` takes only a primary; other agents send the list as `model` as before.
- **Validation** (`checkModelChain`, the daemon stays the authority): bad format, repeat, and unknown provider block Save in the editor; "provider doesn't list this model" only warns. Only lists of two or more are blocked, and entries already saved aren't held against the user, so the old single-model behaviour (typed unlisted ids, a provider since removed) is unchanged.
- **Tests**: client `chain_test.dart`, `remote_test.dart` (urls, probe, redirects); app `servers_test.dart`, `server_switch_app_test.dart` (a switch gives a fresh store and keeps tokens apart), `model_chain_test.dart`, new cases in `agents_test.dart`; screenshots `servers_chain_screenshot_test.dart` (light and dark, looked at). Not run against a real second server or the real Keychain.
- **Review fixes.** A re-paired server gets a fresh token account (`server:<new id>`) and the old account is deleted only after the new write, and `checkServer` re-reads the saved entry after its token read and drops the check if the address, pin or account changed, so a slow Keychain read can't pair an old address with a new token. The Keychain write goes to `security -i` on stdin (quoted, line breaks refused, read back to confirm) so a token is never in `ps`; the Linux token file is written as a 0600 temp file and renamed.

### Script grants: review round

- **Interpreter.** Python locates `pyvenv.cfg`/site-packages/`.pth` from the path it is started from, so the check on the canonical path alone wasn't enough. Now the as-found path, each directory and symlink hop to the target and the target must all be outside the sandbox-writable roots; the canonical file must be a native binary (Mach-O/ELF magic), which refuses asdf/pyenv/mise shims; `call` runs the canonical path, never the found one, and re-resolves it at run time. Tests build a real `.venv/bin/python3` symlink with `pyvenv.cfg`, a writable launcher dir, a symlink hop and a shim script.
- **`-I`.** A grant runs `python3 -I script`, so a `csv.py` planted beside an always-granted stdlib-only script can't run; site-packages still import. Helper modules next to a script don't import under a grant (the card says "the script must be self-contained"). Key version bumped to 2 (old stored keys no longer match: they ask again).
- **Canary off the async path.** `HostBackend::verify` is non-blocking: cache read, else a background probe thread and a transient "still checking" (not cached), with an in-flight marker and no lock held across probes. `verify_now` blocks (doctor, info, audit, tests); the daemon warms the cache at start.
- **Ledger.** `approved_by_grant` is written only when the grant decided the call: not under yolo, not tainted.
- **Replay.** A replayed approval step for a call that never ran (no tool step journaled) and whose tool plans per call (has a grant key) is not trusted: the tool step is journaled as an error ("approved before tern restarted ... make the call again") and the model asks again. Done in the loop, not in the journal's shape, so existing journals are unchanged. Tools without per-call keys keep resuming as before.

### Script grants: review round 3

- **Fail closed when a grant decided the call.** `Tool::grant_applied` tells the tool the host ran the call on a grant. `Shell` marks the call's entry (with the key that decided it); `call` then refuses, running nothing, if there is no plan, the typed command isn't the planned one, or a re-plan no longer yields that key. It never falls through to the typed `sh -c`. A human's answer (allow once, or an answer that created the grant) still runs the typed command as before; a re-plan keeps the mark. Chosen over journaling the key in the approval step: the mark lives with the call's plan and needs no change to the journal shape.
- **Replay.** The restart refusal no longer depends on a key existing now: a replayed approval for a call that never ran is refused when the tool plans per call at all (it has a key or a reason). After a restart the probe cache is empty, so the key is absent exactly when it matters.
- **Interpreter sealed.** `-I` still imports `site`, so the interpreter's own code paths matter: binary directories (found and canonical) and their `pyvenv.cfg` paths, plus what it reports via `-I -S` (prefixes, `sys.path`, site-packages), must be outside the writable roots; the canonical file must be named `python*` (refuses mise). The report is a once-per-file (path+mtime) unsandboxed run with a fixed program, cwd `/`, empty env, `-S` so no `.pth` runs; the directory checks come first. Residual: a writable stdlib dir would run in that one probe.
- **Warming.** There are no per-agent workspaces (every agent shares `workspace`), so the single configured one is warmed at start. At most one probe is in flight per cache key (an in-flight marker; a test counts probes under 16 concurrent callers).

### Script grants: review round 4

- **The inspection runs inside the sandbox.** Round 3 asked the interpreter what it loads by running it unsandboxed, but the stdlib it imports at startup is only known after that run. Now `SandboxBackend::probe_blocking` (host backend: `HostBackend::probe_confined`: Seatbelt/Landlock, network off, no extra write paths) runs the fixed `-I -S` program; with no OS sandbox it errors and no grant is offered ("couldn't inspect ..."; when the sandbox itself isn't confirmed, that is the reason shown). Before any run, a writable root inside the canonical interpreter's prefix (grandparent of the binary, unless that is `/`) is refused. Results are cached per (shell tool, interpreter file, mtime), failures not cached. Cost: one blocking sandboxed run of a few tens of ms on the first script call per interpreter.
- **`.pth` read statically.** Granted runs use `-I` without `-S`, so site-packages `.pth` files run. Each reported site dir's `*.pth` is read at plan time (and again at call, since `resolve` reruns): a path line (relative to the site dir) under a writable root is refused, and any `import` line except the setuptools distutils shim is refused.


### Starter catalog: tighter, plus an SEO auditor

- **Removed `data-analyst`, `bookkeeper` and `tutor`** (and with them the "Data & Finance" category). The catalog is 23 starters including Blank. The `python` tool and script-grant machinery stay: they are general, not analyst-specific. `shell` is now only allowed for the Coding category.
- **Added `seo`** next to `marketing` (Writing & Content). `readonly` ceiling with the `read_only` built-ins (includes `http_fetch`), no connectors, no MCP: it only reads pages and never edits a site. The persona fixes a compact report shape (scorecard, prioritised fixes, "Changed since last run") so a scheduled run is comparable to the last one. It ships a weekly `routine` hint using the existing `routine` field; no schema change. Cost of `readonly`: it cannot save memories, so the "changed since last run" section works from memory the user already has or the previous report in the conversation, and says "first run" otherwise.

### Seeded starter agents

- **Six real agents on a clean install**: assistant, research, code-reviewer, seo, writer-editor, bug-fixer (`SEEDED_STARTERS`), created through `Runtime::create_agent` from the starters, so they carry the starter ceilings and are ordinary agents (edit, archive, delete through every existing path, no protection). Done in `Runtime::open`, which every front end (daemon, CLI, TUI, desktop via the daemon) goes through.
- **Once, ever.** The flag is a `kv` row (`tern`/`starters_seeded`), claimed atomically (`Store::kv_claim`) before anything is created, so two processes cannot both seed and a crash never re-seeds. Deleting the agents leaves the flag (and tombstones), so they stay gone. Migration 0025 is a schema no-op whose hook in `migrate` sets the flag for any database that already existed, so an upgrade never grows agents. As a backstop, seeding is also skipped when any non-default agent row exists, archived or deleted.
- **`TERN_SEED_AGENTS=0`** skips it; it exists for the test harnesses (their homes assert exact agent lists), not as a user setting.
### Guard compares file identity, not path text

- **Why.** On macOS (case-insensitive, normalization-insensitive APFS) `canonicalize` keeps the typed case, so `~/.TERN/SECRETS.TOML` was neither equal to `~/.tern/secrets.toml` nor under `~/.tern`: both the read and write checks were bypassable. A hardlink to a protected file from the workspace was the same hole.
- **What.** `Guard` still does the path comparison, then also stats at check time (the files may not have existed at startup): reads are refused when `(dev, inode)` equals a protected file; writes are refused when the target or any ancestor is the data directory by identity, when the target is a hardlink (nlink > 1) to a protected file or any file in the data directory (bounded scan), and when any component is `.git` in any case. Tests cover typed case, hardlink, symlink chain, and NFD names (macOS only; the case test skips itself on case-sensitive filesystems). `search_files` uses the same read check per file. The shell has no guard check on cwd; it relies on the sandbox.
- **Not done.** Writes are still check-then-open by name (TOCTOU); the capability-handle design in docs/alpha-workspaces.md supersedes that.
### One platform Settings surface

- **Decision.** Every platform-wide setting is edited in one place: the desktop **Settings** window (⌘,) and the TUI `/settings` screen, with the same sections (General, Models & Providers, Secrets, Permissions & Sandbox, Limits & Budgets, Servers, Advanced, About) and a search over all of them. It replaces the separate Models (⌘,), Secrets, Configuration and Settings entry points. **Agent settings are excluded and unchanged**: the agent sheet, per-agent model, tools, autonomy ceiling and channels are edited where they were. `docs/settings-inventory.md` lists every setting, where it was editable before, whether it needs a restart, and whether it is secret.
- **One API, extended.** The daemon's `GET/PUT/DELETE /v1/config/settings` already described every scalar `config.toml` key (type, default, range, security note, live or restart) and wrote with comments kept and validation before saving, so it stays the only path; nothing new was added beside it. Each setting gained `group` (which Settings section), `advanced` (a tuning knob, shown under Advanced) and `restart_required`, and the view gained `restart_required`, so the desktop and the TUI place things identically from the daemon's word instead of two hand-kept lists. `autonomy` became a `choice` (it was free text) and is flagged risky for `autonomous` and `yolo`. Placement lives in one table (`config::settings::placement`) with a test that every key lands in a known group and every user-facing one has a description.
- **Not duplicated.** The providers editor (including the default model and fallback chain editor), the secrets editor and the servers editor are the same widgets as before with an `embedded` mode (no window chrome, title or Done) rather than copies; the old dialogs remain as their standalone form, used where a screen shows one by itself (the agent sheet's "Secrets" link, tests). Every other call site that opened the Models dialog (`conversation`, `items`, `sidebar`, the menu) goes through `openPreferences(context, store, section)`, which finds the `PreferencesScope` that `Home` provides and falls back to the old dialog when there is none. `default_model` is edited by the chain editor, so its config row is hidden in Models; the same setting stays searchable and in the raw file. The security audit row moved from General to Permissions & Sandbox.
- **What is not a `config.toml` setting.** Launch at login and what closing the window does (desktop only), saved servers (desktop `desktop.json`, tokens in the keychain), and the data location and log level (`TERN_HOME`, `TERN_LOG`, environment only: About shows them, nothing edits them) are shown where they belong. There is no telemetry and no updater, and About says so rather than offering a switch for either.
- **TUI.** `/settings [section|text]` is a panel like `/secrets`: sections on the left, rows on the right, Enter toggles, cycles or opens a text field, `r` resets, `/` searches. It reads and writes through two new `Backend` methods (`config_view`, `change_setting`) implemented by the embedded runtime and the remote client; a backend without them says so. Rows for providers, secrets and MCP servers open the existing `/setup`, `/secrets` and `/mcp` panels and closing one comes back. This closes the TODO above about a TUI panel.
- **Tests**: `config::settings::tests` (groups, restart flags, autonomy choice and comment-keeping write), `crates/tern/tests/config_api.rs` (groups and flags over HTTP, validation leaving the file alone), `app::tests::settings` in tern-tui, `preferences_test.dart` (sections, embedded editors, search, restart, 403, about, light/dark screenshots), the client's decoding test.
## Memory for read-only agents (2026-11-05)

The owner decided that agents whose autonomy ceiling is `readonly` (research, SEO, fact-checker, meeting prep) should
be able to read and write their own memory, even though they can't touch files or run commands: memory is not a
system or file write.

- **A class, not a write.** `approval::MEMORY_TOOLS` (`memory_save`, `memory_forget`) are allowed by `check` before
  autonomy or taint are looked at, so a read-only run can use them and no run is asked. The alternative, making
  `memory_save` an `Effect::Read`, would have lied about the tool in lists and the ledger and let a tainted run's
  `Read` fall under the host-limit logic. `profile_edit` is deliberately outside the class: `memory.md` is part of
  every system prompt, so writing it is a persistent instruction channel and stays a refused write for read-only runs.
  Not asking replaces approval with bounds, because asking about every note made read-only agents' memory useless
  and a tainted run's `ask` was a poor defence (the user can't judge a memory in a card, and an unattended run's goes
  to the outbox unread).
- **Own namespace.** `MemScope::of` derives the namespace from the run's frozen agent, memory scope, autonomy and
  taint: a private agent's own rows; the default agent's the shared pool; a read-only or tainted run of any
  non-default agent its own rows even if its memory is `shared`. `shared: true` is refused to those runs. A sub-agent
  already inherits its parent's agent (so the parent's namespace, by design: the sub-agent prompt says it saves "to
  your memory") and a delegate runs as its own agent, so no path writes another agent's memory. `memory_forget` takes
  an id and checks the namespace in the same statement as the delete, so another agent's ids aren't revealed.
- **Caps.** `[memory]`: `entry_max_chars` 2000, `run_max_writes` 20, `max_entries` 500, `max_chars` 200000. Counting
  a run's writes uses the ledger (`memory.save` and `memory.forget`), not the rows, so delete-and-save loops don't
  escape the cap; one transaction checks, writes and records. Chosen over a token cap: a character count is exact and
  needs no tokenizer. Defaults are generous for a note-keeping agent and small enough that a flood stops in one run.
- **Provenance.** `memories.source` (`agent` | `unverified` | `user`) and `run_id` (migration 0026; numbered after the
  starter seed's 0025, renumber on merge). Taint is read from `runs.taint` at call time, which includes the run's
  starting taint. Recall wraps entries in `<memory_recall>` with a note, indents entry text so it can't forge a
  header, defuses a closing tag, and sets `ToolResult.untrusted` when any entry is unverified: the loop then wraps it
  as `<untrusted>` and taints the reader, so a persistent injection that is recalled costs the next run its writes
  until the user says otherwise. A user edit sets `source = user`. Older rows default to `agent`: they were saved in
  clean runs or with the user's approval. A tainted run may forget only unverified notes, so injected text can't wipe
  trusted ones (costs: it can't clean up a clean note it finds wrong; it says so in its report).
- **Audit.** The ledger already recorded every non-read call that ran as `effect`; the memory tools add `memory.*`
  entries written inside the store transaction (with the source and namespace), plus `memory.refused` for refusals, and
  the user's own edits (`memory.edit|delete|clear`, actor `owner`). Both exist for a model write: the generic one
  says a write ran, the specific one says what it was.
- **Routine carry-over.** Routine runs share a session, so earlier turns can be in context, but compaction and
  `NO_REPLY` make that unreliable, and the starter's "what changed" can't rely on the model's own notes. Each run of a
  routine now gets the newest finished, non-quiet report of the previous run appended to its input
  (`Manager::with_carryover`), framed `<previous_run_report>`, capped at `routine_carryover_chars`. If that run was
  tainted the block is wrapped as untrusted and the run starts tainted, because the report may carry what a page said.
  This changes existing routines slightly: a routine that read the web last time starts tainted this time (writes ask
  from the first call, not only after the first fetch). Chosen over a new table: the message is already journaled, so
  replay is exact and nothing needs migrating.
- **UI.** The desktop side panel's existing Memory section gains edit (click), delete, clear and an `unverified`
  marker; `tern agents memory <agent>` is the terminal's (the TUI has no agent management; its `/agents` lists
  sub-agents). The API gained `PATCH` and `DELETE` on `/v1/memories`, in the `chat` scope with the rest of memories.
- **Not done.** No automatic expiry of old or unverified notes, no per-note "verify" action beyond rewriting, no
  separate cap for unverified notes, and the starters' prompts were not changed to mention memory.

## Proactive delegation across agents (2026-11-05)

Most of the machinery existed (`agent_spawn {agent}`, a delegates list in the prompt, ceilings copied to children).
What was missing: the model had no cap on the roster, no ceiling in it, a soft policy, and starter agents (explicit
`builtins` lists, no `delegates`) could not delegate at all, so a persona hint to hand off would have been inert.

- **Roster.** `agent_frame` ranks the other active agents by word overlap between the new run's input and each
  agent's name and "Your job" line (then most recently edited), caps both the "Agents" list and the delegation
  list at `ROSTER_CAP` = 12 with an `...and N more` line, and adds `at most <ceiling>` to each delegate line. It is
  recomputed at every run start (prompts are frozen per run, so a created, archived or deleted agent shows up on
  the next run with no cache to invalidate). Framed in the prompt as data the user wrote.
- **Policy.** One short "Handing work to your agents" section replaces the old paragraph: check the list before
  substantial work, delegate what is clearly in a lane, say so in one line first (a visible step, not silent), brief
  fully, summarise and credit, don't delegate trivia or loop, never delegate to get around a limit. The "ask the user
  to message the other agent" fallback now applies only when the agent can't hand over itself.
- **Plumbing.** An agent with a non-empty delegation list gets the `agent_*` tools in its frozen scope even if its
  scope lists only some built-ins; this adds no reach because the child runs under the delegate's own scope and the
  lower of its ceiling and the caller's autonomy (already true; now covered by a test where a read-only agent
  delegates to a supervised one). Seeded starters get `delegates` = the other seeded starters at seed time (editable;
  user-created agents still start with none). Starter personas gained a one-line hand-off rule where a natural peer
  exists; the rule is inert for peers the user doesn't have.
- **Not done.** Ranking is lexical, not semantic; the persona rule names starter slugs that may not exist in a
  user's home; the "Let agents decide" router is unchanged. The TUI labels a hand-off from the agent id suffix
  (`tern:seo` -> `@seo`), which equals the slug unless the agent was renamed after creation.

## CI cost rules (2026-11-05)

macOS runner minutes cost about 10x Linux, so: Linux runs the full OS-independent suite on every code change; macOS
runs only macOS-specific checks (clippy for cfg(macos) code, Seatbelt/path/tui crates, the shipped aarch64 binary,
platform-sensitive Dart tests), and only after Linux is green, on main pushes, release checks, manual dispatch,
nightly, or PRs labelled `ci-macos`. The universal DMG is built only on dispatch, nightly and release tags. Newer
pushes cancel older runs on the same ref, but never tag (release) runs. Docs-only changes skip everything except
gitleaks. Every job has a timeout (macOS 30). A release tag still requires the full Linux suite and the macOS job to
pass. Details and expected minutes: docs/ci-cost.md.
